People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Like even if I was reading or sketching, I’d start talking to myself, put music on, check something, or turn it into some bigger thing instead of just… doing it calmly.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or be physically active to the point where it was difficult for you to stop as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember it as like… constant running or climbing exactly. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I try to relax by reading or doing a calm sketching exercise, I end up talking to myself, playing music, checking something, or turning it into a bigger project instead of just being quiet with it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don’t have classes or a formal schedule, I still jump from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening a new dataset, like resting feels weird unless I’m actively doing something.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues makes me really antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, or wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: When people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea or start adding to their Figma board before they’ve finished explaining, and I can tell afterward that I came off as pushy even though I was excited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote comments about me rushing through worksheets, losing track of directions, and talking over classmates during group work. Like with friends or people I might collaborate with. I’ve missed out on some opportunities, like small freelance things and a portfolio competition, because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough. And now some people are kind of hesitant to work with me, even if they like my ideas.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble at work, school, or in your personal life because of acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like major trouble, but I’ve definitely made things awkward. In brainstorming, I’ll sometimes blurt out an idea before someone’s finished, or start adding to their Figma board too soon. Afterward I realize I probably came off kind of pushy, even if I was just excited.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.
